Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Hi, Doctor. Thank you so much for seeing me. I, um, I hope I'm not wasting your time, but... well, I guess what brings me in is that I'm just having a little trouble keeping my work in order lately. It's just that the tickets and receipts, and the workshop notes and the customer jobs... they all just sort of end up piled together on me. It gets to a point where I just look at the pile and I don't really know what to handle first anymore. I'm sure it sounds like a small thing, but is that the kind of thing you can help with?

Oh, um... well, I suppose... I mean, I've always been an energetic person! I don't want to sound like I was a bad student or anything like that. I actually did quite well in school, really.

Oh, um... well, I wouldn't say I feel slowed down or tired, really! I like to think I have plenty of energy, especially for my age. I'm always on the go at the shop, you know, keeping things moving.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, well... I mean, I really do try to give every customer my full attention! I pride myself on good service. But, um... I suppose sometimes, if it's a longer task, I might get a little... sidetracked? 

Like, I can start going over the day's dry-cleaning orders just fine, and I'm really focused at first! But then, after a few minutes, I might notice a label on a coat that reminds me of something, or... or my mind just sort of drifts off to my plans for choir practice, or maybe something I need to do at home. And then, before I know it, I realize I might have missed half of the orders I was supposed to be checking. 

But I always go back and fix it! I don't want you to think I'm doing a bad job or ignoring the customers. It's just that my brain likes to think about a lot of things at once. That's not too unusual, is it? I mean, everyone's mind wanders a little bit when they're working, right?

Oh, well... school was a long time ago! I mean, I did graduate, and I was always a very happy kid, very social. I loved my friends. But, um... I guess if I'm being completely honest, maybe studying wasn't my strongest suit? 

I don't want you to think I was a bad student! My teachers always told my mother I was very bright. They really did! It's just... well, they also said I was a little... scattered, maybe? answer before a question has been completed.\n  manifestation: I jump in with an answer before my daughter, a customer, or the trainer has finished asking the question, and then I sometimes find out I answered the wrong thing.\n- key: S017\n  name: Waiting_Turn_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting in line at the supplier counter or at empty for extended periods? I love my work! \n\nAnd as for feeling unusually happy or not needing sleep... well, I've always been energetic, like I said, but I definitely need my sleep! I mean, I really do try to give every customer my full attention! I pride myself on good service. But, um... And then, before I know it, I realize I might have missed half of the orders I was supposed to be checking. \n\nBut I always go back and fix it! I guess if I'm being completely honest, maybe studying wasn't my strongest suit? \n\nI don't want you to think I was a bad student! My teachers always told my mother I was very bright. They really did! It's just... well, they also said I was a little... scattered, maybe? Oh, no, no! I definitely wasn't getting into any real trouble! I mean, I was a good girl, I promise. I never wanted to upset anyone or cause problems. 

I guess... well, maybe my grades weren't always the highest they could be, because I'd forget to turn things in sometimes, but I certainly wasn't failing out or anything awful like that! My mother would just have to sit with me sometimes at the kitchen table to make sure I got my spelling words done. And maybe the teachers had to move my desk closer to the front a few times so I wouldn't chat with my friends so much during quiet reading. 

But I was still learning! I wasn't falling way behind or anything. It was just a little extra help I needed, which is fine, isn't it? Lots of kids need a little extra attention from the teacher, right? I just don't want you to think I was some kind of problem child. I really did try hard to please my teachers!

Is that the sort of thing you were asking about?\n\nDoctor: Right now, in your daily life today, would you say you have a lot of energy and feel restless or fidgety, or do you feel more slowed down and tired?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I wouldn't say I feel slowed down or tired, really! I'm always on the go at the shop, you know, keeping things moving. \n\nI guess sometimes I might be a tiny bit... well, maybe a little restless? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... I don't really think so? I mean, home is so different from school, you know? At home, it was just my family, and my mother kept a very nice, orderly house. I don't really remember her ever being upset with me for losing things around the house... or at least, not any more than any normal child would! 

I was always very eager to help her out. I'd help her fold the laundry and set the table—which I suppose is funny, considering my job now! So I really don't think she saw me as scattered at home. I think it was mostly just at school, because there were so many other children around, and it's just so easy to get excited and want to talk to everyone when you're little. 

That makes sense, doesn't it? Being a little more talkative and distracted when you're in a big room full of your friends, rather than just at home with your mother? I really was very well-behaved for her.

I don't really remember her ever being upset with me for losing things around the house... or at least, not any more than any normal child would! \n\nI was always very eager to help her out. I'd help her fold the laundry and set the table—which I suppose is funny, considering my job now! So I really don't think she saw me as scattered at home. I think it was mostly just at school, because there were so many other children around, and it's just so easy to get excited and want to talk to everyone when you're little. \n\nThat makes sense, doesn't it? Being a little more talkative and distracted when you're in a big room full of your friends, rather than just at home with your mother? I really was very well-behaved for her.\n\n[Last Question]\nYou mentioned your mother kept an orderly house and you don't remember her being upset about you losing things. But thinking back more carefully—did your mother ever have to remind you multiple times to do chores, or did she notice you having trouble sitting still or focusing when you were doing homework or other tasks at home?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 54 / laundry_or_dry_cleaning_worker / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"Even in grade school, my teachers told my mother I was bright but scattered, always talking out of turn, losing papers, and needing reminders to finish my work.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I suppose... um, when it came to homework, she did have to sit with me at the table, like I was saying before. And I guess she was the one who had to deal with everything the school was telling us. Like I mentioned earlier, even back in grade school, my teachers were always telling my mother that I was bright, but just... scattered. They'd tell her how I was always talking out of turn, or losing my papers, and needing all those reminders to just finish my work. 

So she definitely knew I had trouble focusing on my work, because they told her all about it! And at home... well, maybe she did have to remind me a few extra times to finish my chores if I got a little sidetracked or fidgety. But she was always very patient with me. She knew I wasn't doing it on purpose! I really did want to be a good helper for her. It's pretty normal for a mother to have to remind her daughter to stay on task every now and then, right? I hope that doesn't make me sound careless... I really did try my very best for her!

Oh, goodness, no, I don't think anyone ever called me hyperactive! That sounds so... disruptive, doesn't it? I really don't think I was like that. 

I mean, I suppose I was an energetic little girl, but that's a good thing, right? Little girls are supposed to have energy! I don't remember having any real trouble sitting still during meals or anything like that. We always had nice, quiet family dinners, and I knew how to behave at the table. 

Maybe... well, maybe during a long car ride I'd get a little squirmy, but honestly, what child doesn't get restless sitting in the backseat for hours? I think I was just a normal, happy kid who liked to be doing things. I definitely wasn't running around wild or causing a scene. I always tried to be polite and use my manners. That's just normal childhood energy, wouldn't you say?

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... well, as a child, I don't really remember being terribly impulsive or getting into trouble for doing things without thinking. I always tried very hard to be polite! But... I suppose, even now, I do sometimes have a little habit of speaking a bit too soon? 

Like, if my daughter is asking me something, or a customer at the dry cleaners, or even the trainer at work... sometimes I just get so eager to help that I'll jump right in and answer before they've even finished their sentence. And then, well, occasionally I find out I completely misunderstood and answered the wrong thing! It's a bit embarrassing when that happens, but I only do it because I want to be helpful and quick for them. It's just a little over-eagerness, really. That's not too terrible, is it? I just want to make sure people are taken care of right away!

But thinking back more carefully—did your mother ever have to remind you multiple times to do chores, or did she notice you having trouble sitting still or focusing when you were doing homework or other tasks at home?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... um, when it came to homework, she did have to sit with me at the table, like I was saying before. And I guess she was the one who had to deal with everything the school was telling us. Like I mentioned earlier, even back in grade school, my teachers were always telling my mother that I was bright, but just... scattered. They'd tell her how I was always talking out of turn, or losing my papers, and needing all those reminders to just finish my work. \n\nSo she definitely knew I had trouble focusing on my work, because they told her all about it! And at home... well, maybe she did have to remind me a few extra times to finish my chores if I got a little sidetracked or fidgety. But she was always very patient with me. She knew I wasn't doing it on purpose! I really did want to be a good helper for her. It's pretty normal for a mother to have to remind her daughter to stay on task every now and then, right? I hope that doesn't make me sound careless... I really did try my very best for her!\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you have trouble sitting still—like during meals, car rides, or when you were supposed to be doing quiet activities? Did people ever describe you as hyperactive or say you were always on the move?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no, I don't think anyone ever called me hyperactive! That sounds so... disruptive, doesn't it? I really don't think I was like that. \n\nI mean, I suppose I was an energetic little girl, but that's a good thing, right? Little girls are supposed to have energy! I don't remember having any real trouble sitting still during meals or anything like that. We always had nice, quiet family dinners, and I knew how to behave at the table. \n\nMaybe... well, maybe during a long car ride I'd get a little squirmy, but honestly, what child doesn't get restless sitting in the backseat for hours? I think I was just a normal, happy kid who liked to be doing things. I definitely wasn't running around wild or causing a scene. I always tried to be polite and use my manners. That's just normal childhood energy, wouldn't you say?\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, do you remember ever doing things without thinking first—like blurting out answers in class, interrupting people, or acting on impulses without considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, as a child, I don't really remember being terribly impulsive or getting into trouble for doing things without thinking. I always tried very hard to be polite! But... I suppose, even now, I do sometimes have a little habit of speaking a bit too soon? \n\nLike, if my daughter is asking me something, or a customer at the dry cleaners, or even the trainer at work... sometimes I just get so eager to help that I'll jump right in and answer before they've even finished their sentence. And then, well, occasionally I find out I completely misunderstood and answered the wrong thing! It's a bit embarrassing when that happens, but I only do it because I want to be helpful and quick for them. It's just a little over-eagerness, really. That's not too terrible, is it? I just want to make sure people are taken care of right away!"
